Proposition 23.2 (Linking operation). Let A be an Ed -algebra in cochain complexes. Evaluation of
the binary little-disks operation on the fundamental cycle of S d−1 defines

                             {−, −} : H p (A) ⊗ H q (A) −→ H p+q+1−d (A).                          (151)

Together with the product induced by a point of the binary little-disks space, this operation gives the
canonical Pd -algebra structure on H • (A).

Proof. The space of binary little d-disks is homotopy equivalent to S d−1 . Evaluation on its fundamental
class has cohomological degree 1 − d. The codimension-one faces in the compactification of the three-
disk configuration give the biderivation and shifted Jacobi identities; their determinant orientations
give the standard Pd signs.

    For an Ed -chiral algebra, (151) belongs to the transverse topological structure. The diagonal-
supported chiral operation on X simultaneously carries the holomorphic OPE. The interchange law
follows from the defining requirement that every transverse multiplication map be a morphism of
factorisation objects.

23.5    Higher centres
For A ∈ AlgEn (CX ), define the higher centre ZEn (A) by its universal property: it is the final En+1 -
algebra equipped with a central action on A. Under the presentability hypotheses above, the centre
exists and agrees with the En Hochschild cochain object

                                         ZEn (A) = HHE• n (A).                                     (152)

For n = 1, this is the Hochschild cochain object RHomA⊗Aop (A, A) with its E2 structure. The higher
Deligne theorem equips (152) with an En+1 multiplication.
Theorem 23.3 (Higher open–closed recognition). Let B be an En+1 -algebra and let A be an En -algebra.
Every central action of B on A determines a unique En+1 morphism

                                            B −→ ZEn (A)                                           (153)

compatible with the action. A protected boundary condition is complete precisely when this morphism
is an equivalence for its protected local bulk algebra B.

Proof. The first assertion is the representing property that defines ZEn (A). Completeness means
that a protected local bulk operation is determined uniquely by its action on the boundary. The
representing morphism is therefore fully faithful and essentially surjective exactly in the complete
case.

23.6    The dimensional ladder
The three operations associated with one additional transverse direction are now explicit:

                   integration over a pointed disk            7−→ iterated bar,
            passage from boundary to universal bulk action 7−→ higher centre,
                   integration over a linking sphere          7−→ shifted Poisson bracket.

                                                  109
R. Lorgat                                                                Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


The chiral coefficient on X remains fixed throughout. Higher transverse dimension acts on the En
structure, while diagonal collision continues to be expressed by the same factorisable D-module
geometry.


24     BRST descent and W -algebras
24.1    A differential compatible with chirality
Let (C, Q) be a cochain complex equipped with a chiral operation X. The statement that Q is a
derivation of the chiral structure is
                                         [Q, X] = 0                                     (154)
in the chiral deformation Lie algebra. If Q2 = 0 and [X, X] = 0, then

                                         [Q + X, Q + X] = 0.

Thus the BRST differential and the chiral operation form one Maurer–Cartan element with unary
and binary parts.

Proposition 24.1 (BRST descent of chiral operations). If Q is a square-zero derivation of a chiral
algebra C, then HQ (C) inherits a chiral algebra structure. The induced operation is independent of
the choice of cocycle representatives.

Proof. Equation (154) says that the operation sends cocycles to cocycles and that changing one input
by a boundary changes the output by a boundary. The chiral Jacobi identity descends because it
holds before taking cohomology.

   The proposition applies equally to vertex algebras in a formal coordinate. It is the algebraic core
of Hamiltonian reduction.

24.2    The BRST–collision bicomplex
The collision complex of C carries the internal differential induced by Q and the collision differential
induced by X. They anticommute by (154). Hence
                                                                    
                                             CIcoll (C), Q + dcoll                                         (155)

is a bicomplex.
    Filter by BRST degree. The first spectral sequence has

                                         E1 ∼
                                            = CIcoll (HQ (C)).

Filter by collision degree. The other spectral sequence computes BRST cohomology of the collision
resolution.

Theorem 24.2 (Bar–BRST comparison). Assume that the tensor products and sums forming the
coefficient objects are Q-acyclic outside degree zero, and that the total bicomplex (155) is bounded
below in one grading and complete with respect to an exhaustive filtration in the other. Then the



                                                     110
R. Lorgat                                                        Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


canonical map of collision complexes
                                                                        
                                 CIcoll (HQ
                                          0
                                            (C)) −→ HQ
                                                     0
                                                       CIcoll (C), Q                               (156)

                              j
is an isomorphism, while HQ     (CIcoll (C), Q) = 0 for j ̸= 0. Consequently the total complex is quasi-
isomorphic to CIcoll (HQ
                       0 (C)) with its induced collision differential.


Proof. Taking vertical Q-cohomology commutes with the coefficient tensor products under the stated
acyclicity hypothesis, which proves (156) and the higher vanishing. The BRST-first spectral sequence
therefore has one nonzero row. Boundedness and completeness identify its abutment with the total
cohomology, giving the final quasi-isomorphism.

   The theorem is exact and strong, but its concentration hypothesis is substantive. In the general
case the result is the full derived BRST bar object; concentration identifies it with the ordinary bar
complex of degree-zero cohomology.

24.3    Quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction
Let g be a simple Lie algebra and f ∈ g nilpotent. Quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction forms a
BRST complex from the affine vertex algebra Vk (g), charged ghosts, neutral fields when required, and
a differential determined by f . The affine W -algebra is

                                    W k (g, f ) = HBRST
                                                   0
                                                        (CDS
                                                          k
                                                             (g, f )).                             (157)

For the universal W -algebra, the BRST cohomology is concentrated in degree zero in the standard
construction; the resulting vertex algebra has an associated graded described by functions on an arc
space of the Slodowy slice [2, 3].
   The chiral operation on the BRST complex descends by Theorem 24.1. The complicated nonlinear
OPEs of W -generators are therefore transferred collision operations. Their composite fields are
chiral-geometric operations; they are the output of homological reduction.

24.4    Homotopy transfer
Choose a contraction
                           i
                    (H, 0) ⇄ (C, Q),      Qi = 0,     pQ = 0,    Qh + hQ = id − ip.
                           p

Homotopy transfer expresses the induced higher operations on H as sums over rooted trees. Vertices
carry the original chiral operation, internal edges carry h, leaves carry i, and the root carries p.
   If the transferred structure is strict, the tree sums beyond the binary operation cancel. In the
general transferred model, H carries a homotopy chiral structure. The cohomology classes of the
higher operations measure the obstruction to strictification; their raw coefficients depend on the
contraction.
   In Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction, the cohomology is known independently to carry a strict vertex-
algebra structure. A P∞ quasi-isomorphism from the transferred model to the strict BRST-cohomology
model identifies the two Maurer–Cartan elements by gauge equivalence. If the contraction extends



                                                    111
R. Lorgat                                                         Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


to a P∞ quasi-isomorphism from the transferred homotopy chiral structure to the strict BRST-
cohomology model, then the two Maurer–Cartan elements are gauge equivalent and the transferred
higher operations are removable in that model. Before choosing that comparison, the higher operations
form the canonical transfer data attached to the contraction.

24.5    Feigin–Frenkel duality for principal W -algebras
For principal nilpotent f , the Feigin–Frenkel duality relates the principal W -algebras of Langlands
dual Lie algebras. With lacing number r∨ , the levels k and ℓ satisfy

                                       r∨ (k + h∨ )(ℓ + L h∨ ) = 1.                                 (158)

Under this relation there is an isomorphism

                                           W k (g) ∼
                                                   = W ℓ (L g).                                     (159)

This is the standard W -algebra duality. It is the multiplicative shifted-level relation of principal
W -algebra duality, distinct from the additive affine reflection k 7→ −k − 2h∨ .
   The duality may be constructed through screening operators or free-field realizations. Its relation to
a bar coalgebra requires a separate comparison between the BRST resolution and the chiral Chevalley
construction.

24.6    Screening operators
A free-field realization embeds a W -algebra into an intersection of kernels of screening operators. The
screenings are contour integrals of vertex operators and commute with the W -algebra up to total
derivatives. Their kernels form a complex whose cohomology realizes the reduced algebra.
   Geometrically, a screening contour is a cycle in a configuration space. Its monodromy and
intersection pairing depend on the charges of the free fields. Multiple screenings produce hypergeometric
local systems and quantum-group actions. This is an ordered configuration-space realization of BRST
reduction.

24.7    Critical level
At k = −h∨ , the Sugawara stress tensor is undefined because its denominator vanishes. On the formal
disc D, the center of the critical vacuum vertex algebra is

                                     Z(V−h∨ (g)) ∼
                                                 = O(OpL G (D)) .                                   (160)

The center of the completed critical enveloping algebra over the punctured disc is described instead by
functions on opers on D× , with the appropriate completion and central-character formulation [34, 36].
These are related but distinct statements. The vertex center is a commutative chiral algebra and
underlies the local geometric Langlands construction.
   The critical level is singular for KZ because ℏ = (k + h∨ )−1 diverges. The parameter q =
exp(πi/(k + h∨ )) becomes singular in this normalization. The affine chiral algebra remains well defined,
but its conformal structure and monodromy description change character.



                                                   112
R. Lorgat                                                         Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


24.8    What BRST reduction preserves
BRST reduction preserves any structure represented by a chain map commuting with Q. It preserves
the chiral operation, module actions, and pairings that satisfy this condition. The following structures
are preserved after the corresponding chain maps commuting with Q have been supplied:

  • a chosen quadratic presentation;

  • Koszulness of that presentation;

  • a particular braid local system;

  • a Verdier-dual identification;

  • positive-genus sewing before compatibility with Q is proved.

   The precise theorem is Theorem 24.2. Every stronger statement is a comparison theorem for the
corresponding additional structure.

24.9    The physical meaning
Classical Hamiltonian reduction imposes constraints and divides by gauge transformations. BRST
replaces this quotient by a cochain complex whose differential combines constraints and ghosts.
Quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction is the chiral version. The Virasoro or W -algebra is the algebra
of gauge-invariant boundary observables after reduction.
   From the field-theoretic viewpoint, the ghost central charge is part of the anomaly calculation.
BRST nilpotence requires cancellation of the total anomaly in a coupled theory. This is an equality of
central extensions in the BRST complex, a tensor-product cancellation of the anomaly classes in the
BRST complex.


25     Critical level, opers, and the derived Langlands fibre
At critical level the affine current algebra acquires a large commutative chiral centre. Feigin and
Frenkel identify this centre with functions on opers for the Langlands dual group. A central character
is therefore a geometric point, and its derived fibre is calculated by the Koszul complex of the oper
conormal directions.

25.1    The Feigin–Frenkel centre
Let g be a complex simple Lie algebra, let h∨ be its dual Coxeter number, and set

                                          Vcrit (g) = V−h∨ (g).

Let L G be the Langlands dual group and let D = Spec C[[t]].

Theorem 25.1 (Feigin–Frenkel). There is a canonical isomorphism of commutative chiral algebras

                                     Z(Vcrit (g)) ∼
                                                  = O(OpL G (D)) ,                                  (161)

where OpL G (D) is the ind-scheme of L G-opers on the formal disc [34, 36].

                                                  113
R. Lorgat                                                                 Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


   If d1 , . . . , dr are the exponents of g, the centre has topological generators of conformal weights
di + 1. In a formal coordinate, their Fourier modes identify the centre with the classical W -algebra
attached to L g. Coordinate independence is built into the oper description: an oper is a connection
together with a Borel reduction satisfying the oper transversality condition.

25.2    A regular central fibre
The local derived calculation is the Koszul resolution of a regular character.

Proof. Resolve the residue field Cχ over each polynomial jet algebra ZN by the Koszul complex on a

a finite window makes higher Tor vanish and leaves the ordinary quotient in degree zero.

25.3    Change of rings
The oper directions enter Ext through a standard change-of-rings spectral sequence.

B = Aχ . For left B-modules M and N there is a natural convergent spectral sequence

                        E2p,q = ExtpB (M, N ⊗ Λq Tχ Spec Z) =⇒ ExtA
                                                                  p+q
                                                                      (M, N ).                         (167)

The exterior factor is the tangent space dual to the conormal generators of (164).

Proof. Apply the Cartan–Eilenberg change-of-rings spectral sequence for A → B:

                               ExtpB M, ExtqA (B, N ) =⇒ Extp+q
                                                            A (M, N ).
                                                         


Compute ExtqA (B, N ) by applying HomA (−, N ) to the Koszul resolution (162). The central sequence

                                    ExtqA (B, N ) ∼
                                                  = N ⊗ Λq Tχ Spec Z.

   Applying the theorem to each oper-jet truncation gives a tower of spectral sequences. Surjective
transition maps and finite-dimensional weight windows identify the completed spectral sequence with
the inverse limit of the finite ones.

25.4    Gaudin Hamiltonians from the centre
Choose pairwise distinct points z1 , . . . , zn and finite-dimensional g-modules V1 , . . . , Vn . Evaluation
of the critical centre on V1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Vn produces the Gaudin commutative algebra. The quadratic
Segal–Sugawara generator maps to

                                                 Ωij
                                   Hi =                      [Hi , Hj ] = 0.                           (168)
                                          X
                                                     ,
                                              z − zj
                                          j̸=i i

Higher central generators produce the higher Gaudin Hamiltonians. The spectrum of this commutative
algebra is described by L G-opers on P1 with the corresponding singularity data, and Bethe eigenvectors
arise from Miura opers [83].



                                                    115
R. Lorgat                                                         Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


   The two proofs of commutativity expose the same geometry. The critical centre is commutative
before evaluation. Equivalently, the infinitesimal braid relations make the Gaudin connection flat, and
residues of a flat commuting family commute.

25.5    Local and global geometric Langlands
The critical centre supplies the local spectral base. Frenkel and Gaitsgory identify the spherical
critical-level category with quasi-coherent sheaves on the ind-scheme of unramified opers:

                                  bcrit - ModG[[t]] ≃ QCoh Opunr   ×
                                                             L G (D ) .                             (169)
                                                                       
                                  g

The equivalence is the spherical local geometric Langlands theorem [84].
   For a smooth projective curve C, a global L G-oper ρ defines a critically twisted D-module on
BunG (C) by Beilinson–Drinfeld localization. The resulting object is a Hecke eigensheaf with eigenvalue
equal to the L G-local system underlying ρ [81]. The local centre (161) and the global localization
functor therefore form the geometric chain

                 critical central character ρ 7−→ localized twisted D-module on BunG ,
                                                                                                    (170)
                localized twisted D-module 7−→ Hecke eigenvalue underlying ρ.

25.6    The three compatible derived directions
A critical ordered chiral theory can carry three commuting differentials:

  (i) the chiral Chevalley differential of affine-current collision;

 (ii) the interval-bar differential of a specified transverse E1 multiplication;

(iii) the oper-Koszul differential of a selected central character.

Linearity of the transverse multiplication over the Feigin–Frenkel centre makes the three structure
maps commute. The total complex is then trigraded by chiral collision degree, interval length, and
oper-conormal degree. Filtering in the three possible directions produces spectral sequences for one
derived object.
   In the Gaudin realization, the oper is the joint spectral datum, the evaluated central generators
are commuting quantum Hamiltonians, and the Miura-oper fibre encodes Bethe eigenvectors. The
evaluation homomorphism from the critical centre to the Gaudin algebra is the exact mathematical
map relating the chiral and integrable descriptions.

R. Lorgat                                                            Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


Part IV
Two genera and two master equations
26     Two genera and the two-edge master equation
26.1    Two loop-forming operations
Let A be an ordered chiral algebra. A cyclic trace on its transverse E1 multiplication closes an interval
into a circle and produces Hochschild chains, annuli, ribbon graphs, and open topological surfaces. A
modular sewing structure on the chiral coefficient glues marked points of algebraic curves by plumbing
and produces conformal blocks. Both operations contract pairs of flags, but their moduli spaces and
anomaly lines are different.

Definition 26.1 (Transverse Calabi–Yau structure). A transverse Calabi–Yau structure of degree d⊥
is a trace
                                        FactD(X)
                                tr⊥ : HH0        (A) −→ 1[−d⊥ ]
whose pairing ⟨a, b⟩⊥ = tr⊥ (ab) is perfect and cyclic.

Definition 26.2 (Chiral modular sewing structure). A chiral modular sewing structure of degree dX
consists of perfect pairings on the relevant insertion or module objects and operations assigned to stable
pointed complex curves, compatible with separating and nonseparating clutching, the factorisation
D-module maps, and the determinant twists.

  The first definition is an open two-dimensional topological structure internal to the chiral coefficient.
The second varies the complex curve. Their simultaneous presence is additional bicoloured structure.

26.2    Bicoloured stable graphs
A bicoloured stable graph Γ has two sets of internal edges,

                                        E(Γ) = EX (Γ) ⊔ E⊥ (Γ).

An X-edge represents a chiral plumbing contraction. A ⊥-edge represents a transverse cyclic contraction.
Vertices carry the operation appropriate to the incident flags; transverse flags retain their cyclic order.
The orientation line is
                                 or(Γ) = det(EX (Γ)) ⊗ det(E⊥ (Γ)).                                  (171)
Contracting a loop of colour X raises the chiral genus g by one; contracting a loop of colour ⊥ raises
the ribbon genus h by one.
   Let Gbi be the groupoid of these graphs with isomorphisms preserving the edge colour and cyclic
orders. The free completed graph object generated by the two sewing systems is

                                 Fbi (A) =             (or(Γ) ⊗ A(Γ))Aut(Γ) .                          (172)
                                              Y

                                             [Γ]∈Gbi


   This definition directly records the two edge colours and their common vertex operations. A
tensor-product presentation is a further comparison model.


                                                        117
R. Lorgat                                                            Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


26.3    The two boundary operators
Let ∂X be the signed sum of contractions of one X-edge and ∂⊥ the signed sum of contractions of one
⊥-edge.
Proposition 26.3 (Independent graph boundaries). With the orientation convention (171),

                             2
                            ∂X = 0,           2
                                             ∂⊥ = 0,         ∂X ∂⊥ + ∂⊥ ∂X = 0.

Hence ∂X + ∂⊥ is square-zero.

Proof. For two edges of the same colour, the two contraction orders induce opposite orientations in
the determinant of that edge set. For edges of different colours, the final graph is the same in either
order, while exchanging the two odd determinant factors in (171) changes the sign.

   The proposition is a graph-theoretic identity. A geometric realization must additionally show that
the chosen ribbon and Deligne–Mumford boundary maps represent these edge contractions.

26.4    The bicoloured Feynman transform
Let PX be the modular or cyclic object governing chiral sewing, and let P⊥ govern transverse cyclic
sewing. Assume that their endomorphism actions on A obey the interchange identities on common
flags. Define the two-edge Feynman transform to be the free completed bicoloured graph object with
differential
                                       dF = dint + ∂X + ∂⊥ .
By Proposition 26.3, this is a differential.
  The corresponding completed convolution complex has components
                                                                              
                            A =           HomΣn P bi (g, h, n), EndA (g, h, n) ,                       (173)
                                  Y
                           gbi
                                  g,h,n

completed by the number of internal edges and vertices. There are brackets [−, −]X and [−, −]⊥ from
joining two vertices with an edge of the indicated colour, and loop operators ∆X and ∆⊥ from joining
two flags of one vertex.
Theorem 26.4 (Two-edge master equation). A morphism from the two-edge Feynman transform to
the cyclic endomorphism object of A is equivalent to an element

                                              Θbi
                                               A ∈ gA [[ℏX , ℏ⊥ ]]
                                                    bi


satisfying
                       1 bi bi       1 bi bi
                    A + [ΘA , ΘA ]X + [ΘA , ΘA ]⊥ + ℏX ∆X ΘA + ℏ⊥ ∆⊥ ΘA = 0.
                  dΘbi                                                                                 (174)
                                                           bi         bi
                       2             2
Proof. The free transform is generated by corollas, and its differential is the sum of the internal
differential and all one-edge contractions. Evaluating a morphism on the generators gives Θbi    A.
Compatibility with dF is exactly (174). Conversely, the equation extends the assignment on corollas
uniquely to a chain morphism on the free completed graph object.

   The hypotheses supply both sewing systems, and the theorem identifies their common master
equation.

                                                       118
R. Lorgat                                                     Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


26.5    The two loop operators
On the suspended convolution complex, both loop contractions are odd.

Proposition 26.5 (Supercommutation of loop contractions). With the determinant convention (171),

                                         ∆X ∆⊥ + ∆⊥ ∆X = 0.

After passing to unsuspended scalar amplitudes, the induced even contractions commute.

Proof. Applying the two contractions in either order produces the same bicoloured graph and the
same coefficient contraction. The two new edge-orientation factors occur in opposite orders in the
determinant line. Since each loop operator is odd on the suspended convolution complex, interchanging
those factors contributes a minus sign. Desuspension removes the two odd shifts and yields commuting
even scalar contractions.

   At bidegree (g, h) = (0, 1), one sees the annulus and the Hochschild trace. At (1, 0), one sees
genus-one chiral sewing and its projective anomaly. At (1, 1), both loop operations occur; the mixed
codimension-two identity is their supercommutation.

26.6    Geometric realization
A geometric realization of the ⊥-edges uses ribbon surfaces or cyclically ordered boundary configu-
rations. A realization of the X-edges uses stable curves and clutching morphisms. Compatibility is
expressed by a Cartesian or correspondential model in which the two degenerations commute through
the prescribed interchange map.

Criterion 26.6 (Geometric two-edge realization). A pair of geometric sewing theories realizes the
two-edge master equation when:

  (i) every codimension-one boundary of either type has a coefficient contraction;

 (ii) same-colour codimension-two boundaries satisfy the usual modular or ribbon boundary identity;

(iii) mixed codimension-two boundaries form Cartesian comparison squares and carry the product
      orientation (171);

(iv) the coefficient contractions commute over those squares;

 (v) the graph filtration is complete.

Proof. Conditions (i) and (ii) identify each same-colour geometric face with the corresponding one-edge
contraction and make its square vanish. Conditions (iii) and (iv) identify the two orders of a mixed
degeneration with one Cartesian boundary stratum and the product-orientation sign, which gives
anticommutation. Completeness in (v) makes the graph sum convergent in the edge filtration. Hence
the geometric boundary differential maps to dF and Theorem 26.4 applies.




                                                 119
R. Lorgat                                                              Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


26.7    Open topological field theory and chiral conformal theory
A smooth proper Calabi–Yau E1 algebra defines an oriented open topological field theory; its value on
the circle is Hochschild homology,     Z
                                                    A ≃ HH• (A).
                                               S1
Ribbon graphs compute its amplitudes. This is the g = 0 row with arbitrary h.
   A chiral conformal theory assigns blocks to complex curves and varies with complex structure
through a projectively flat connection. This is the h = 0 column with arbitrary g.

Separation theorem 26.7 (Independent genus gradings). The universal parameter space of the
combined theory is bigraded by (g, h). The coordinate g counts nodes and handles of algebraic curves;
the coordinate h counts handles of transverse ribbon surfaces. A string duality relating the two
expansions is represented by a specified map of the corresponding modular sewing theories.

Proof. The two graph genera are changed by contractions of different edge colours and are preserved
separately by every remaining operation. They therefore define a genuine Z2≥0 grading on (172).
Identifying the two expansions requires a morphism that sends the X-coloured sewing maps to
combinations of the transverse ribbon sewing maps and respects their master equations.

26.8    The double expansion
For a matrix realization, a connected ribbon graph is weighted by a power of N determined by h and
the number of boundaries. Chiral sewing is weighted by a string coupling determined by g. When
both exist, the connected potential has the form

                               FA (N, gs ) =           N 2−2h−b gs2g−2 Fg,h,b (A).                       (175)
                                               X

                                               g,h,b


The coefficients are traces of the bidegree components of Θbi
                                                           A . Keeping the two parameters separate
is the exact algebraic expression of the distinction between matrix-planar and worldsheet-genus
expansions.

26.9    Duality of the two-edge theory
Theorem 26.8 (Duality of bicoloured amplitudes). Suppose:

  (i) A and A! are joined by an invertible bimodule Morita context on the selected complete perfect
      sector;

 (ii) their transverse Calabi–Yau traces correspond;

(iii) their chiral sewing systems are identified by a tensor equivalence preserving projective connections,
      pairings, and determinant twists.

Then the equivalence induces an isomorphism of two-edge convolution complexes carrying Θbi
                                                                                        A to ΘA! .
                                                                                              bi


Proof. Morita invariance identifies Hochschild chains, ribbon contractions, and ∆⊥ . The sewing
equivalence identifies the chiral contractions and ∆X . Preservation of determinant lines identifies the
orientation signs. Hence every term of (174) is intertwined.

                                                        120
R. Lorgat                                                       Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


   A numerical complementarity statement follows only after a trace has been applied to this structured
equivalence.

26.10    Physical meaning
The operator ∆⊥ contracts two open or line-defect states and creates a loop of the transverse topological
theory. The operator ∆X contracts two chiral insertions through a plumbing fixture and creates a
handle on the complex curve. Their supercommutation is locality for two independent degenerations.
The universal object is the bicoloured graph operation recording the contracted edge and the moduli
space that carries it.


27      Cyclicity, stable graphs, and the master equation
27.1    Why genus requires a pairing
A genus-zero operation has outputs and inputs. To create a loop, one must identify two flags. Such an
identification requires a pairing. Let V be a complex with a nondegenerate graded-symmetric pairing

                                        ⟨−, −⟩ : V ⊗ V −→ C[d]                                     (176)

that is invariant under the genus-zero operations. The pairing converts an output into an input and
permits contraction along internal edges.
   A cyclic pairing as in (176) contracts two flags and defines nonseparating sewing. A cyclic chiral
algebra, a conformal-block pairing, or a field-theoretic propagator supplies this datum and thereby
produces the modular object.

27.2    Modular operads
A modular operad has operations indexed by genus g and a finite set of legs. It allows two kinds
of composition: joining a leg of one operation to a leg of another, and joining two legs of the same
operation. The first adds genera; the second raises genus by one. Stable graphs encode iterated
compositions [43].
   Let P be a dg modular cooperad and let Endmod
                                               V   be the endomorphism modular operad determined
by the pairing (176). The completed convolution space
                                                                       
                              gP,V =         HomΣn P(g, n), Endmod
                                                               V (g, n)                            (177)
                                       Y

                                       g,n

contains a differential, a bracket joining two vertices, and a loop operator ∆ joining two legs of one
vertex.

27.3    The Feynman transform
The Feynman transform F P is the free modular operad on the shifted dual of P, with the standard
determinant twists, and with differential given by the internal differential and the signed sum over edge
contractions. A morphism F P → Endmod   V    is determined by its values on generators. Compatibility
with the differential is the master equation.


                                                    121
R. Lorgat                                                         Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


Theorem 27.1 (Feynman transform and master equation). Giving V the structure of an algebra over
the Feynman transform F P is equivalent to giving an element Θ ∈ gP,V satisfying

                                            1
                                        dΘ + [Θ, Θ] + ℏ∆Θ = 0.                                         (178)
                                            2
Proof. The free modular operad is generated by corollas. The internal part of its differential gives
dΘ. Contracting an edge between two vertices gives the bracket term, with the factor 1/2 accounting
for interchange of the vertices. Contracting a loop at one vertex gives ∆Θ. Equality with the
endomorphism differential is exactly (178).

   The theorem is formal and unconditional. Its hypothesis is the existence of a morphism from
the Feynman transform, equivalently the operations that Θ is supposed to encode. The constructed
sewing maps assign operations to the boundary strata, and boundary-of-boundary proves their master
equation.

27.4    Stable curves
The boundary strata of Mg,n are indexed by stable graphs. Separating nodes correspond to edges
joining distinct vertices. Nonseparating nodes correspond to loops. Clutching morphisms realize graph
contraction geometrically.
   Borel–Moore chains on the clutching spaces, with the usual determinant twists, form a modular
operad under proper pushforward along gluing. After aritywise finite duality, or in a specified
completed continuous dual, the corresponding cochains form a modular cooperad. If a system of
multilinear maps on V is compatible with every clutching morphism, it defines an algebra over the
appropriate Feynman transform. The master equation then states that the two ways of reaching every
codimension-two boundary stratum cancel with orientation signs.

Principle 27.2 (Sewing principle). The topology of Mg,n supplies the relations among sewing maps.
The coefficient theory supplies the multilinear maps assigned to the clutching strata.

Proof. The cellular or Borel–Moore boundary of the stable-curve stratification is the signed sum of
one-edge graph contractions, and its square vanishes. A coefficient system becomes an algebra over the
Feynman transform by assigning a compatible contraction to every such edge. Applying the coefficient
assignment to the boundary identity gives the relations among these maps. Thus topology supplies
the relations and the coefficient theory supplies their values.

27.5    Graph filtration and obstruction theory
Filter gP,V by the number of internal edges, or more generally by stable-graph complexity. The
filtration is complete and compatible with the bracket. Suppose a genus-zero Maurer–Cartan element
Θ0 is fixed. A partial lift
                                      Θ≤r = Θ0 + Θ1 + · · · + Θr
has curvature in filtration r+1. Its leading term is a cocycle for the twisted differential dΘ0 = d+[Θ0 , −].

Theorem 27.3 (Filtered obstruction theorem). The obstruction to extending Θ≤r to filtration r + 1
is a canonical class                                        
                             or+1 (Θ≤r ) ∈ H 2 grr+1
                                                 F gP,V , dΘ0 .                             (179)

                                                    122
R. Lorgat                                                         Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


It vanishes exactly when an extension exists. When it vanishes, the set of extensions modulo gauge
transformations trivial in lower filtration is a torsor for the corresponding H 1 .

Proof. This is the standard obstruction theory of a complete filtered Maurer–Cartan equation. The
Bianchi identity implies that the leading curvature is closed. Changing a lift alters it by a coboundary.
Solving the next equation is equivalent to choosing a primitive; two primitives differ by a closed
degree-one element.

   This graph obstruction tower is invariant. A morphism from the modular deformation complex to
a primary-line deformation complex transports the bracket and produces a functorial scalar tower.

27.6    Connected graph cumulants
Suppose a cyclic trace assigns a scalar weight W (Γ) to every stable graph and the graph sum converges
formally in ℏ:
                                                X ℏg(Γ)
                                       Z =1+                W (Γ).                               (180)
                                                Γ
                                                   | Aut Γ|
The exponential formula for combinatorial species gives

                                                           ℏg(Γ)
                                   log Z =                        W (Γ).                            (181)
                                                X

                                             Γ connected
                                                         | Aut Γ|

Thus connected amplitudes are cumulants of the full partition function. This is a canonical scalar
projection once the trace is fixed. The complete chiral algebra is retained by the untraced graph-valued
master element.

27.7    Graphwise duality
Let V and V ∨ be paired cyclic objects. Transposing every vertex operation and using the inverse
pairing on every internal edge sends a graph weight for V to a graph weight for V ∨ . Orientation and
determinant lines contribute the standard signs.

Proposition 27.4 (Graphwise transpose). Assume that the coefficient systems are aritywise perfect,
that continuous duality commutes with the stable-graph completion, and that the cyclic pairing identifies
their endomorphism modular operads. Then transposition at every vertex and contraction with the
inverse pairing on every edge induce a filtered isomorphism between the two modular convolution
complexes. It intertwines their master equations and sends connected graph weights to their transposes.

Proof. Aritywise perfection identifies every vertex-operation space with the dual operation space. The
inverse cyclic pairing identifies the contraction assigned to each internal edge, and the determinant line
transports its sign. Tensoring these identifications over the vertices and edges of a stable graph gives
an isomorphism on each graph summand. Compatibility with completion assembles the graphwise
maps into a filtered isomorphism. Edge contraction and transposition commute, so the differential,
bracket, and loop operator are intertwined; the master equation and connected traces follow.

   Any scalar complementarity formula is a trace of this graphwise statement and depends on the
chosen duality and trace. Levels, central charges, and Hodge classes acquire scalar relations only after
a specified graphwise duality and trace map sends them to a common target.

                                                   123
R. Lorgat                                                       Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


27.8    The Batalin–Vilkovisky reading
In the BV formalism, fields form a shifted symplectic space, the bracket is induced by the symplectic
pairing, and ∆ is the second-order operator corresponding to contraction of a loop. The quantum
action S satisfies
                                         (Q + ℏ∆)eS/ℏ = 0,                                     (182)
which expands to
                                            1
                                      QS + {S, S} + ℏ∆S = 0.
                                            2
This is the same algebraic shape as (178). Equality of the two equations for a particular theory requires
a map from the renormalized BV complex to the modular endomorphism complex. Compactified
configuration spaces supply that map in perturbative models where the Feynman weights extend to
the boundary.

27.9    Anomalies
The degree-one quantum-master defect is the local anomaly cocycle. Its cohomology class is independent
of the choice of counterterm. An anomaly can be cancelled by adding a local counterterm exactly
when the class vanishes. A projective connection on conformal blocks is a different but related
manifestation: the curvature is central rather than zero. Tensoring by a line with opposite curvature
converts projective flatness into flatness.
   The next section treats this anomaly line geometrically on the moduli space of curves.


28     Conformal blocks, sewing, and the anomaly line
28.1    Coinvariants and blocks
Let V be a conformal vertex algebra and let M1 , . . . , Mn be modules inserted at marked points of a
smooth curve C. The global chiral algebra acts on M1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Mn . The coinvariants are the quotient by
this action; conformal blocks are their duals. Varying the pointed curve produces sheaves on moduli.
   Damiolini–Gibney–Tarasca construct sheaves of coinvariants for vertex algebras on moduli of pointed
stable curves and prove factorization and vector-bundle results under finiteness and semisimplicity
hypotheses [22, 24]. For strongly rational vertex operator algebras, Damiolini–Woike prove that the
conformal blocks define a modular functor [25].
   The modular functor is the positive-genus coefficient system required by Theorem 27.2. Its genus-
zero restriction is built from intertwining operators and the vertex tensor category. Its sewing maps
are convergent analytic operations and satisfy the mapping-class-group relations.

28.2    Projective flatness
The sheaf of conformal blocks carries a projectively flat connection. Infinitesimal changes of complex
structure act through the stress tensor. The central term in the Virasoro algebra makes the action
projective. Algebraically, the sheaf is a module for an Atiyah algebra rather than for the untwisted
tangent sheaf.
   Let λ = c1 (E) be the Hodge class and ψi the cotangent-line classes at the markings. If ai is the



                                                  124
R. Lorgat                                                          Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


conformal weight of Mi , the interior projective class has the form
                                                               !
                                               c
                                         rk(V)   λ+                                                   (183)
                                                    X
                                                      ai ψi .
                                               2    i

On the compactification, boundary terms are determined by factorization through intermediate
modules. Their coefficients depend on fusion rules and conformal weights [22, 23].

Theorem 28.1 (Chern-class form of the anomaly). For a conformal-block bundle satisfying the
factorization hypotheses, its first Chern class is the sum of the interior class (183) and explicit
boundary divisor contributions obtained by sewing through simple modules. In particular, the Hodge
coefficient is rk(V)c/2, and the boundary sewing coefficients supply the remaining divisor classes.

Proof. The Atiyah algebra acting on conformal blocks is the sum of the Hodge Atiyah algebra with
coefficient c/2 and the cotangent-line Atiyah algebras with coefficients ai . Taking the trace of projective
curvature gives the interior term (183). Along a boundary divisor, factorization decomposes the bundle
into summands indexed by the simple intermediate module; the residue of the logarithmic connection
on each summand is its conformal-weight sewing exponent. Summing the traces of these residues gives
the boundary divisor coefficients. This is the Chern-class calculation of Damiolini–Gibney–Tarasca
[22, 23].

   This theorem separates the local central charge from the global geometry. Two theories with the
same central charge may have different ranks, conformal weights, fusion rules, and boundary classes.

28.3    The anomaly line
A projectively flat bundle E has curvature

                                               ∇2E = Ω idE

for a scalar two-form Ω. Let L be a line bundle with connection satisfying ∇2L = −Ω. Then

                                                ∇2E⊗L = 0.

If only a rational multiple of the required line exists, one works with a twisted D-module or on the
corresponding gerbe.

Proposition 28.2 (Anomaly cancellation by a line). Tensoring a projectively flat conformal-block
system by a line or twisted line of opposite Atiyah class produces a flat system. The construction is
compatible with sewing when the anomaly lines satisfy the clutching multiplicativity dictated by the
Hodge and cotangent-line bundles.

   The vertex-algebra differential remains fixed, while the proposition twists the connection on the
family of blocks over moduli.

28.4    Sewing and the master equation
Choose a perfect pairing between a module and its contragredient. Sewing two marked points inserts
the inverse pairing and sums over a homogeneous basis. In a local sewing parameter q, the operation

                                                    125
R. Lorgat                                                            Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


contains the propagator
                                                 q L0 −c/24 .
Convergence is analytic; factorization identifies the limit at q = 0 with the direct sum over intermediate
modules.
   The modular-functor sewing maps define an algebra over the chains of the surface operad. After
the anomaly twist of Theorem 28.2, they determine a solution of the master equation in the modular
convolution algebra. This conclusion follows from Theorem 27.1; the coefficients are exactly those
carried by the sewing action and anomaly line.

28.5    Genus one
At genus one, Zhu’s theorem gives modular transformation laws for trace functions of rational vertex
operator algebras [78]. For a holomorphic VOA,

                                          ZV (τ ) = TrV q L0 −c/24

is a modular function with the appropriate multiplier. For a rational VOA, the vector of irreducible
characters transforms under a finite-dimensional representation of SL2 (Z).
    The projective anomaly appears as the factor q −c/24 and the multiplier system. The modular
S-matrix and fusion coefficients are related by the Verlinde formula when the representation category
is modular [47, 73]. The dimension of a conformal-block space is a trace of the modular functor; the
SL2 (Z) representation is strictly richer.

28.6    Prime forms and analytic kernels
On a compact Riemann surface, the prime form E(z, w) is a canonical section, after the choice of
a theta characteristic, that vanishes simply on the diagonal. The differential dz log E(z, w) has the
correct local simple pole but is quasi-periodic. Adding a harmonic correction produces a single-valued
Green kernel. Fay’s identities control products of prime forms and Szego kernels [32].
   These kernels are analytic representatives of propagators and connections. Their quasi-periodicity
produces monodromy and projective curvature. They define the analytic connection in the base
directions; the internal block differential occupies the fibre degree, and the total superconnection
preserves both gradings.

28.7    Free bosons and determinant lines
For a rank-r free boson, the oscillator partition function is

                                                  η(τ )−r .

The factor η −r is the regularized determinant of the chiral Laplacian, while the nonchiral partition
function includes (Im τ )−r/2 |η(τ )|−2r . On higher-genus surfaces, the determinant line of the ∂¯ operator
carries the Quillen metric and curvature given by the family index theorem.
   This is the precise meeting point of Polyakov’s conformal anomaly and the geometry of determinant
lines. The coefficient is the central charge of the free fields. Interacting theories add non-Gaussian
conformal blocks; their higher correlators are determined by the full OPE, fusion rules, and sewing


                                                    126
R. Lorgat                                                             Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


maps.

28.8    Lattice theories and Siegel theta series
For an even positive-definite lattice L of rank r, the genus-one character of the lattice VOA is

                                                          ΘL (τ )
                                            ZVL (τ ) =            .                                        (184)
                                                          η(τ )r
                                                                           (g)
At higher genus the zero modes contribute the Siegel theta series ΘL (Ω), while oscillators contribute
determinant factors. Equal genus-one theta series refine to lattice or VOA equivalence only after the
full higher-genus theta and OPE data agree equal lattices or equal VOAs.
    For the two even unimodular lattices of rank sixteen, the degree-g Siegel theta series agree for
g ≤ 3. Their degree-four difference is proportional to the Schottky form and vanishes on the Jacobian
locus. Hence their genus-two theta amplitudes coincide. The first ambient Siegel distinction occurs in
degree four, and every arithmetic comparison therefore specifies both genus and moduli locus.

28.9    Genus two and beyond
Genus two introduces a three-dimensional Siegel upper half-space, separating and nonseparating
boundary divisors, and stable graphs with loops. The period matrix and the off-diagonal sewing
parameter couple channels that are independent at genus one. For rational VOAs, the modular functor
gives finite-dimensional blocks with mapping-class-group monodromy. For lattice VOAs, Siegel theta
series provide explicit sections.
   The stable-graph genus expansion is built from the complete vertex tensors and propagators. The
central charge controls the anomaly line; fusion and OPE coefficients control the non-scalar graph
weights.


29      Anomalies, curvature, and invertible field theories
29.1    An anomaly has a domain
The word anomaly denotes an obstruction to making a classical symmetry act strictly after quantization.
Its mathematical type depends on the symmetry and on the object on which the symmetry acts. The
following classes occur in the present subject:

            central extension of a local operator algebra,                       [ωloc ] ∈ HLie
                                                                                            2
                                                                                                (s; C),
            projective curvature of a family of states,                          [Ωfam ] ∈ H 2 (B; C),
            BV quantization obstruction,                                         [ABV ] ∈ Hloc
                                                                                           1
                                                                                               (Obscl ),
            BRST obstruction,                                                    [Q2 ] ∈ H 2 (End C),
            framing anomaly,                                                     αfr ∈ π0 InvFTfr
                                                                                               d+1 ,
            curvature of a curved Koszul object,                                 m0 ∈ C 2 .

The symbols lie in different complexes. A theorem can identify their images under transgression or a
comparison map. A scalar comparison arises after a specified transgression or trace maps these classes
to a common one-dimensional target.

                                                   127
R. Lorgat                                                              Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


29.2    Local central extensions
The affine current algebra has central term

                                                      k(a, b)   J [a,b] (w)
                                  J a (z)J b (w) ∼            +             .
                                                     (z − w)2    z−w

The Virasoro algebra has central term

                                               c/2      2T (w)     ∂T (w)
                             T (z)T (w) ∼            +           +        .
                                            (z − w)4   (z − w) 2   z−w

The parameters k and c classify different central extensions. The Sugawara construction relates them
for a chosen affine theory:
                                                   k dim g
                                           cSug =          .
                                                   k + h∨
This rational function is the Sugawara central charge and has a pole at the critical level; the affine
level remains the coefficient of the current central extension.
   A central extension is part of the chiral operation. Invariance of its cocycle makes the central term
compatible with composition. The simplicial identities of the ordered bar and the Jacobi identities of
the chiral coefficient then place the central term inside a square-zero total differential.

Proposition 29.1 (Central terms and the ordered bar). Let A be an augmented dg E1 algebra in
FactD(X) whose chiral coefficient carries a central extension. The ordered bar coderivation satisfies
b2 = 0. The central term contributes to b through the multiplication of A or through its coefficient
differential; its contribution is part of the square-zero simplicial bar coderivation.

Proof. The simplicial identities of the bar construction use only the associativity and chain-map
property of multiplication. A central extension satisfying the algebra identities is already included in
that multiplication. Hence the standard proof of b2 = 0 is unchanged.

29.3    Projective curvature over moduli
Let V → B be a bundle of conformal blocks. A projectively flat connection satisfies

                                               ∇2V = Ω idV .

The class [Ω] is the curvature of a line-valued central extension of the tangent action on B. For pointed
stable curves, the interior contribution to the first Chern class is
                                                                   !
                                              c
                                        rk(V)   λ+
                                                   X
                                                     ai ψi ,
                                              2    i

and factorization supplies boundary terms. The central charge controls one coefficient of this class;
the conformal weights and fusion channels control the rest.

Theorem 29.2 (Type of the conformal anomaly). The conformal anomaly of a family of chiral blocks
is the central extension encoded by the Atiyah algebra of the block bundle. After a projective connection
is chosen, the same class is represented by its scalar curvature. It acts in the moduli direction while
the internal differential on each block complex remains square-zero.

                                                      128
R. Lorgat                                                           Chiral Koszul duality for ordered fields


Proof. The internal differential is defined before the base curve varies and has cohomological degree
one in the fibre. The projective curvature is a two-form on the base acting by a scalar endomorphism
of the fibre. They belong to the vertical and horizontal parts of the superconnection. The family
Ward identity identifies the horizontal curvature with the central extension of vector fields represented
by the stress tensor.

29.4    The superconnection equation
The correct object combining fibre and base is a superconnection

                                    D = dfib + ∇ + A2 + A3 + · · · .

Its square has total degree two. Flatness means D2 = 0; projective flatness means D2 = Ωid.

Proposition 29.3 (Cancellation by an anomaly line). Let DV be a projectively flat superconnection
with D2V = Ω idV . If a line bundle L with connection satisfies ∇2L = −Ω, then the tensor-product
superconnection on V ⊗ L is flat. If [Ω] is rationally integral, the same construction takes place in the
gerbe of twisted bundles determined by the required denominator.

Proof. The tensor-product superconnection is

                                      DV⊗L = DV ⊗ 1 + 1 ⊗ ∇L ,

where the second summand acts with the Koszul sign. The two odd summands supercommute, so

                                   D2V⊗L = Ω idV⊗L − Ω idV⊗L = 0.

For a rationally integral class, local root lines and their descent isomorphisms define the corresponding
twisted tensor product; the same curvature calculation holds on every chart and is preserved by
descent.

   The cancellation datum consists of the line or gerbe, its connection, and the curvature identification
used in the proof.

29.5    Polyakov’s Weyl anomaly
Let g = e2σ ĝ be two metrics in the same conformal class on a closed surface. Fix the convention
W [g] = − log Z[g] and
                                           1   √

                                           2 Σ
For a conformal field theory of central charge c, the Weyl anomaly integrates to
                                                 c

up to a metric-independent topological constant. Differentiation at σ = 0 gives
                                                          c
